24 · Human controller interface at 5 ms

Historical snapshot. Read this in its original stage. Later reports supersede earlier inventory and milestone claims. Original Markdown file.

Human controller interface at 5 ms

15 October 2026. This is the implementation contract for the new isolated human trackers. It describes what must be preserved when integrating them downstream; it does not claim that a shared dynamic human/G1 scene is already validated. Per-checkpoint acceptance results are in report 22.

Clock and action contract

Quantity Required behavior
Physics integration One actual MuJoCo-Warp step of 0.005 s
Actor inference Every fourth physics step: 0.020 s / 50 Hz
Held actor output 48 joint-angle residual commands; same output held for four steps
Reference and PD drive Recomputed every 5 ms, using the current reference phase and actual state
Residual bounds Clip raw action to [-1,1], multiply by checkpoint scale, currently 0.25 rad, then clamp the resulting target to joint limits
Root Free and unactuated; no applied root wrench or pose correction
Phase Elapsed time since the single guard reset; advance by 5 ms per integrated step

The residual is held for 20 ms; the reference and resulting drive/torque are not held constant for that whole period. A 20 ms actor period also does not add an artificial 20 ms sensor or actuator delay. Learned contact-timing error is measured separately.

At an actor boundary the current implementation refreshes derived kinematics, builds the observation, and obtains a new residual. It then performs:

repeat four times:
    sample reference pose, velocity and feedforward at the current phase
    build the bounded joint-position target using the held actor residual
    write the native actuator drive
    integrate exactly one 5 ms physics step
    advance the reference clock by 5 ms

Simulation.forward() refreshes FK, contacts and forces; it does not integrate time or replace qpos/qvel. New checkpoints use this refreshed observation convention. The original 2 ms checkpoint used a different derived-state timing convention and must not be silently reinterpreted.

Evaluation records nominal timestamps, the simulator's own per-world clock, physics-step count and actor-update indices. Acceptance checks require monotonic 5 ms simulator increments and actor updates every four steps, allowing the documented floating-point clock tolerance. There are no hidden 2 ms steps.

Actuator input is a drive, not an applied torque

The new runtime uses one native, unit-gear affine actuator for each hinge:

drive = kp * target_angle + kd * reference_velocity + joint_feedforward
actual_torque = clip(drive - kp * actual_angle - kd * actual_velocity,
                     original_minimum_torque, original_maximum_torque)

ctrl contains the drive. It must not be clipped to the old direct-motor torque range: the large kp * target_angle term is cancelled inside the native force law. The physical output is bounded by actuator_forcerange, with the unchanged original strength limits. Audit actuator_force and the corresponding qfrc_actuator entries, rather than interpreting ctrl as torque.

The native damping derivative is included in implicitfast; the installed Warp implementation suppresses that derivative when the output is already saturated. No additional passive damping, armature, strength or external support was introduced. Reference support feedforward is an offline joint torque calculation, not an applied support force. The optional joint balance feedback setting is part of the checkpoint contract; the primary new trackers use it disabled.

Cached forces recorded immediately after a physics step describe the preceding integration interval, while qpos/qvel are the new state. Recomputing force equivalence must respect that pairing. Independent geometric contact metrics recompute FK from the recorded state; foot-support evidence uses the recorded actual contact forces. These recordings do not establish biological muscle or impact-force calibration.

Artifact and state compatibility

Keep the checkpoint together with its exact reference, body metadata, controller_schema.json, source-body XML and exported runtime XML. The source body has 55 qpos coordinates, 54 velocity coordinates and 48 actuators. Map named joints and their qpos/DOF addresses explicitly when placing it inside a larger scene; local array offsets are not universal scene offsets.

The runtime XML must preserve native affine bias, force-limit flags and solver settings. Use export_runtime_model(...) from scalar_control.py, which recompiles and checks the export. The earlier mj_saveLastXML serialization failure is documented in report 20.

Checkpoint validation covers the model/reference hashes, joint order and addresses, gains, strength limits, action scale, observation layout, physics and actor periods, playback speed, hold duration, feedforward, balance-feedback setting, controller mode and FK-refresh convention. Changed timing, actuators or observations require explicit transfer/retraining and new evaluation. A successful tensor load alone is not compatibility evidence.

Each current network is specialized to one family and its reference. The four reference clips have different initial guards and stances. Accepted isolated evaluation means one initial reset followed by an uninterrupted attack and recovery. It does not authorize resetting between punches inside a continuous game or switching networks from an arbitrary posture. A transition/command interface still needs to be implemented and validated for continuous play.

What the G1 integration still needs

Read-only inspection confirms that robust_sav's standing, stance and snapshot contracts use 5 ms physics, decimation 4 and 20 ms control. See robust_sav/src/boxing/snapshots/artifacts.py and robust_sav/src/boxing/snapshots/restoration.py. The new human clock matches that required cadence.

Matching clocks does not make the other global simulator settings identical:

Global setting Current human tracker Recorded G1 native recipe
Integrator implicitfast implicitfast
Friction cone Elliptic Pyramidal
Solver Newton Newton
Solver iterations 30 10
Line-search iterations 15 20
Tolerance 1e-7 1e-8

The G1 values come from the checked-in robust_sav/configs/boxing/standing/native_recipe.json; the exact accepted G1 artifact/configuration must be verified at integration time. A shared MuJoCo model has one global cone and solver configuration. Preserve the accepted G1 configuration first, then qualify or adapt the human under that shared configuration. The current human support-force decoder assumes elliptic constraints and also needs adjustment if using a pyramidal cone. The present 5 ms claim does not cover that unperformed solver-configuration comparison.

Integration also requires entity-local observations/actions, independent state/reset ownership, suitable contact/constraint capacity, and tests showing that adding the human does not alter the accepted G1 action/observation contract. Current human references target one fixed head center; moving-target conditioning, perception/latency, punch transitions and adversarial choices remain separate work. Analytic glove/head distance checks and actual impact response must be distinguished when configuring inter-body collisions.

Before accepting a packaged tracker or shared-scene adapter, recompile its runtime XML and compare body mass/inertia, geometry, joint ranges, armature, passive damping and actuator output limits with the frozen source. Also verify controller/reference hashes, actual simulator-clock records, force caps, zero root/external assistance and complete guard-start evaluation results. These checks preserve the demonstrated physical controller while making the remaining integration changes explicit.

The final independent runtime review checks the selected four learned/baseline pairs separately from their full 64-world acceptance audits. Recompiled body properties and original strength caps match exactly. Actual force satisfies the recorded native law to within 0.0000301 Nm, and the reconstructed zero-residual baseline drive matches bit-for-bit in the nominal and last bank worlds for every family. These checks support the isolated controller contract; they do not remove the shared-scene solver and contact-integration work described above.

25 · Reference dynamics: why the hooks need conditioning

Historical snapshot. Read this in its original stage. Later reports supersede earlier inventory and milestone claims. Original Markdown file.

Reference dynamics: why the hooks need conditioning

15 October 2026. This investigation follows the numerically stable 5 ms controller in report 20 and the geometric reference bank in report 21. It helps explain why successful retargeting and planted soles do not guarantee physical tracking. The body, joint ranges and original torque limits remain unchanged.

Diagnostic method and its limits

reference_dynamics_probe.py evaluates the ideal generalized force needed to track each sampled reference exactly:

required_force = M(q) * reference_acceleration + bias(q, reference_velocity)

Acceleration is a finite difference of the saved generalized velocities. A linear program allocates ground forces at eight sole corners to match the six unactuated root-force/moment equations. It minimizes the largest fraction of the original actuator torque limits needed by the remaining 48 joint equations. Normal forces must be nonnegative. Tangential forces obey abs(Fx) + abs(Fy) <= 0.9 * Fz, a conservative diamond inside the circular friction cone. This is an offline calculation; no forces are applied to a simulation and no model parameters change.

This is a conditional necessary-condition diagnostic, not a proof that a punch is feasible or impossible. It assumes exact reference motion and the specified simultaneous foot support. It omits contact compliance, consistency of foot accelerations, passive joint-stop/self-contact reactions and force-rate limits. The friction approximation can reject support that a different cone would allow. Conversely a feasible allocation does not show that a feedback controller can realize it. A learned policy can change the body trajectory while preserving the punch objective.

An allocation failure means that the assumed ground forces cannot realize the reference root wrench at that sample. A torque fraction above 1 means the assumed exact reference needs more motor strength than available even under the best feasible force allocation. For upper-body joints, the sole Jacobian columns are zero: ground-force redistribution cannot directly reduce their required generalized torque. The updated diagnostic reports this demand even at samples where the root-wrench allocation fails. Other passive reactions are still outside that calculation.

Original reference bank

Job 5931716 sampled all four families every third reference frame. Job 5931727 repeated both hooks at every reference frame. The clock multiplier 0.6 scales reference velocities by 0.6 and accelerations by 0.36 at the same poses; it also stretches inserted recovery in this diagnostic. It is not a claim that an unchanged-duration, uniformly slower cycle exists.

Family Reference clock multiplier Peak minimum torque fraction on support-feasible samples Root-support allocation failures Sampling
Jab 1.0 0.838 4 / 78 Every third frame
Jab 0.6 0.449 0 / 78 Every third frame
Cross 1.0 0.555 8 / 77 Every third frame
Cross 0.6 0.561 2 / 77 Every third frame
Left hook 1.0 2.783 14 / 238 Every frame
Left hook 0.6 1.181 0 / 238 Every frame
Right hook 1.0 2.207 10 / 236 Every frame
Right hook 0.6 0.988 3 / 236 Every frame

Low torque fractions in this table must not be read independently of the support failures. For example, the cross's feasible samples need modest motor effort, yet parts of its prescribed root motion still fail the support test.

All four references have approximately 9.6–10.9 cm minimum static COM margin inside the combined sole polygon. Thus the main problem is not simply the COM moving outside the feet. Dynamic acceleration and angular-momentum demand can be inconsistent with foot support even when this static margin looks healthy.

Both curves use reference phase on the horizontal axis so that the same poses align; their real elapsed times differ. Gaps and crosses identify samples with no feasible assumed ground-force allocation. Vector figure.

The overloaded motion is broader than one bad IK sample

The original left hook peaks at 97.4 Nm on LeftShoulder_y near 1.133 s, against its 35 Nm limit. The right hook reaches approximately 77.3 Nm on RightShoulder_z near 1.164 s, also against 35 Nm. Ground-independent upper-body overload occurs at 21 left-hook frames and 17 right-hook frames, roughly 0.35 s and 0.28 s of sampled time. These are sustained parts of the swing, not isolated single-frame glitches.

At a representative left-shoulder-y peak near 1.15 s, the required torque is 90.9 Nm in magnitude. Gravity alone contributes only 9.9 Nm; gravity plus velocity-dependent bias contributes 74.8 Nm, with the remaining 16.1 Nm coming from acceleration. The right-shoulder-y peak has the same pattern. In contrast, the right-shoulder-z peak is mainly acceleration-driven. Fast coupled arm, torso and root motion therefore matters; reducing only one shoulder angle's acceleration cannot remove all of the transmitted demand.

These particular shoulder-y peaks lie inside the anatomical joint ranges, so passive stop reaction does not directly explain them. Detailed decomposition is saved in overload_decomposition.json.

Conditioning attempts without increasing strength

The reference agent tested additional posture regularization while preserving the original bank. All comparisons below use every reference frame at the candidate's original clock:

Candidate Change Left-hook peak fraction Right-hook peak fraction Finding
Original v1 Existing 25 ms smoothing and sole reprojection 2.783 2.209* High dynamic demand
5931729 Shoulder guard-posture prior 0.3 2.373 2.273 Helps left modestly; slightly worsens right
5931733 Shoulder guard-posture prior 1.0 2.366 2.269 Stronger prior provides little further benefit
5931749 Root guard-rotation prior 3.0, shoulder prior 0.3, relaxed wrist-orientation weight 0.05 2.384 2.303 Does not resolve the overload

*The 2.209 value includes the largest ground-independent upper-body demand at a root-support-infeasible frame; the corresponding support-feasible LP maximum is 2.207. This distinction is preserved in the raw results.

The shoulder-prior candidates become much closer to feasible demand at a 0.6 clock multiplier: about 1.05 times the left-hook cap and 1.03 times the right, with no root-support allocation failures. This supports a slower training curriculum, while still requiring actual rollout validation.

A further candidate uses declared nonuniform retiming: shorten some initial preparation and spend more time on the heavily loaded swing while retaining the overall cycle duration. Its local phase rate differs from the source; it must not be labelled untouched uniform 1.0× playback. Reference velocities, accelerations, attack annotations and contact times must all be recomputed on the new clock, and the added phase acceleration must be included in this diagnostic before recommending training.

The first retimed bank, outputs/scalar-hook-retime-v1, borrows 0.25 s from the initial quiet interval. Its local source phase rates vary approximately 0.47–1.38 for the left hook and 0.49–1.37 for the right. All poses, velocities, accelerations and phase/contact annotations are resampled on the new clock. The source path and complete-cycle duration are retained, with small interpolation-induced sole movement recorded by the reference audit.

Full-frame diagnostic 5931826 shows a substantial improvement:

Retimed candidate Peak required strength fraction Root-support failures Interpretation
Right hook 0.7622 0 / 236 All sampled ideal allocations satisfy the original strength bounds; suitable for a physical learning trial
Left hook 1.0448 3 / 238 Much closer, but three shoulder-y samples still exceed 35 Nm and three separate samples lack the assumed root support

The comparison uses actual elapsed time, so the peaks move as well as shrink. The total cycle duration remains unchanged. Crosses mark support-allocation failures, and gaps are not zero motor demand. Vector figure.

The remaining left shoulder-y excess occurs at 0.883–0.917 s and peaks at 36.57 Nm; support failures occur at 0.25, 1.417 and 1.8 s. These intervals guide a smaller follow-up timing adjustment rather than another broad change to the body. The right-hook result is a useful necessary-condition check, not yet evidence that its learned controller succeeds. Actual 5 ms training and independent uninterrupted-cycle evaluation remain required.

Reproducibility and evidence

Inside a CPU Slurm allocation, from the project directory:

PYTHONPATH=src OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 OMP_NUM_THREADS=1 \
  .venv/bin/python -B scripts/reference_dynamics_probe.py \
  --output outputs/my-reference-dynamics \
  --families left_hook right_hook --stride 1 --speeds 1 .6

The script saves its own source, reference hashes, per-frame allocations, summary counts and explicit assumptions. Three tests verify asymmetric torque limits and a known impossible-friction case; all pass. The relevant evidence is:

  • All-family diagnostic and method notes.
  • Full-frame original hooks.
  • Shoulder prior 0.3 and shoulder prior 1.0.
  • Root/wrist conditioning.
  • Nonuniform retiming, every frame.
  • Upper-body demand including support-infeasible frames.

The outcome is guidance for reference conditioning and learning, not permission to relax strength, joint-limit, balance or contact gates. Final acceptance remains a complete learned rollout at 5 ms physics with the original bounds.

Subsequent physical validation

The retimed candidates were then trained with the unchanged bounded 5 ms controller. Early checkpoint failures were specific: neck/shoulder return posture delayed guard recovery, and the left hook briefly hyperextended the right knee. Their recorded-rollout diagnosis shows why targeted posture/limit learning was appropriate; neither higher strength nor relaxed acceptance gates was needed.

The selected retimed hooks subsequently passed all physical gates and the stricter tracking/timing targets in fresh 64-trial banks: left hook and right hook. Matched zero-residual controllers passed 0/64 complete trials for both. The small left-reference overload therefore did not prevent a successful learned motion that could depart slightly from that exact trajectory, illustrating the diagnostic's stated limits. The reference timing remains explicitly nonuniform. These tests cover small initial-state perturbations of the same fixed-target clips; they do not establish general motion or moving-target robustness.
